## Document 470: [產業] 9월 Monthly Check - 하나증권

Source PDF: all report pdf/[產業] 9월 Monthly Check - 하나증권.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026년 10월 02일 I 산업분석_Update 
증권 
9월 Monthly Check

3분기 국내증시 일평균 거래대금 52.4조원(QoQ -41.7%) 기록 
3분기 국내증시 일평균 거래대금은 52.4조원(KOSPI 44.2조원, KOSDAQ 8.3조원)으로 전분기
대비 42% 감소했다. 2026년 1분기 대비로도 21% 감소했는데, 3분기에는 KOSDAQ을 중심
으로 거래가 크게 위축된 영향이 컸다(상반기 KOSDAQ 일평균 거래대금 18.4조원). 9월 일
평균 거래대금은 40.1조원(KOSPI 31.3조원, KOSDAQ 8.8조원)으로 전월대비 19% 감소했다.  
3분기 ETF 일평균 거래대금은 22.9조원(QoQ -18.5%) 기록했다. 2026년 1분기 대비로는 
29% 높은 수준이나, 7월 33.3조원을 기록한 이후 거래대금이 급감했으며, 9월은 16.6조원으
로 전월대비 5.7% 감소하는 모습이 나타났다.  
수급 측면에서는 개인과 외국인이 각각 12.4조원, 22.8조원을 순매도한 반면, 기관과 기타법
인이 각각 3.9조원, 31.4조원을 순매수했다. 기타법인 순매수에는 삼성전자와 SK하이닉스의 
자사주 매입 물량이 포함되어있으며, 양사의 자사주 매입이 막바지에 접어든 만큼 향후 기
타법인 수급 강도는 둔화될 전망이다.  
9/29 고객예탁금과 신용공여잔고는 각각 107.7조원(MoM +8.0%), 59.0조원(MoM +0.4%) 
기록했다. 고객예탁금은 7월 중순 이후 100조원 초반 수준을 유지하고 있으며, 신용공여잔
고 역시 최근 2개월간 큰 변동 없이 유지되고 있다.  
3분기 해외주식 거래규모는 1,249억달러(QoQ -30.7%), 순매수대금은 74억달러 기록했다. 
9월 해외주식 거래규모는 339억달러(MoM -16.0%), 순매수대금은 10억달러 기록했다. 거
래규모는 분기와 월간 기준 모두 2024년 상반기 이후 가장 낮은 수준이다. 9월에는 SGOV 
ETF(2.9억달러), SCHD ETF (2.3억달러) 등 미국 채권 및 배당형 ETF를 중심으로 매수세가 유
입된 게 특징적이었다.  
한편 국고채 3년물과 10년물 금리는 각각 4.01%, 4.41%로 전분기대비 31bp, 32bp 상승했
다(vs. 1Q26 60bp, 50bp).  
단기적으로 QoQ 감익은 불가피하나 배당주 중심의 접근은 유효 
커버리지 증권사의 3분기 순이익은 거래대금 감소와 금리 상승 영향으로 전분기대비 최소
30% 이상 감소할 것으로 예상한다. 실적 눈높이 역시 낮아지고 있으나, 당분간 현 수준의 
거래대금이 이어질 경우 실적 변동성이 상대적으로 낮고 배당 매력도가 높은 종목 중심의 
접근이 유효할 것으로 판단한다. 이에 하반기 Top Pick으로는 기대배당수익률이 7% 초반이
며 KOSDAQ 점유율까지 상승 중인 키움증권을 제시한다(도표 19 참조).  
다만 중장기적으로는 토큰증권 시장 개화에 따른 증권사별 수혜 정도가 차별화될 것으로 예
상한다. BK·IB 경쟁력과 글로벌 네트워크를 기반으로 신규 사업에 적극 진출할 수 있는 증권
사의 경쟁력이 부각될 전망이다.

Overweight

Compliance Notice  투자등급 관련사항 및 투자의견 비율공시

 당사는 2026년 10월 2일 현재 해당회사의 지분을 1%이상 보유 하고 있지 않습니다  
 본 자료를 작성한 애널리스트(고연수)는 자료의 작성과 관련하여 외부의 압력이나 부당
한 간섭을 받지 않았으며, 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 신의성실 하게 작성하였
습니다. 
 본 자료는 기관투자가 등 제 3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
 본 자료를 작성한 애널리스트(고연수)는 2026년 10월 2일 현재 해당회사의 유가증권을 
보유하고 있지 않습니다

 투자의견의 유효기간은 추천일 이후 12개월을 기준으로 적용 
 기업의 분류 
BUY(매수)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 15% 이상 상승 여력 
Neutral(중립)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 -15%~15% 등락 
Reduce(비중축소)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 -15% 이상 하락 가능 
 산업의 분류 
Overweight(비중확대)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 15% 이상 상승 여력 
Neutral(중립)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 -15%~15% 등락 
Underweight(비중축소)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 -15% 이상 하락 가능 
투자등급 BUY(매수) Neutral(중립) Reduce(매도) 합계 
금융투자상품의 비율 98.18% 1.82% 0.00% 100% 
* 기준일: 2026년 10월 02일

본 조사자료는 고객의 투자에 정보를 제공할 목적으로 작성되었으며, 어떠한 경우에도 
무단 복제 및 배포 될 수 없습니다. 또한 본 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사가 신뢰할 만한 
자료 및 정보로 얻어진 것이나, 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없으므로 투자자 자
신의 판단과 책임하에 최종결정을 하시기 바랍니다. 따라서 어떠한 경우에도 본 자료는 
고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재의 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다.

자료: 에프앤가이드, QuantiWise, 하나증권 
 자료: 에프앤가이드, QuantiWise, 하나증권

1. 화장품 3분기 실적 전망(3): 펌텍코리아/동국제약 
1) 동국제약: 화장품 수출 B2B 확대 중 
 3분기 매출과 영업이익은 각각 전년 동기 대비 12%, 5% 증가한 2,540억원과 260억원(OPM 10.2%)으로 거의 2분기와 
유사할 수준이 될 것으로 보인다. 시장 기대치(OP 272 억원)에는 다소 못미칠 것으로 예상되는데, 아마존 프라임데이 
매출에도 불구하고 헬스케어 매출이 QoQ로 감소하고, 동국생명과학 실적 부진이 지속되고 있기 때문이다. 동국제약 
본사의 경우 OTC와 ETC 매출이 YoY 5%, 헬스케어 부문 매출이 YoY 29% 증가하면서 실적 개선을 견인할 전망이다. 
헬스케어 부문 매출에서 화장품 비중은 80%이다. 화장품 수출 600억원을 목표로 시작했는데, 2분기에만 500억원 가까이 
달성, 연간 수출 천 억원 달성은 무난해 보인다. QoQ 매출이 감소하는 이유는 여전히 국내 사업 비중이 70%를 넘는 상황, 
상대적으로 기초 화장품 비수기였기 때문이다. 
 4분기 아마존 블랙프라이데이, 일본 메가와리 행사 효과를 기대하고 있다. 오프라인, B2B 사업을 올해 본격적으로 
시작했는데, 수출에서 B2B 매출 비중은 30%에 이른다. 미국은 코스트코(약 200개 점포), 얼타뷰티(1,400개 점포), 
노드스트롬(89개 점포)에 입점했는데, 리오더 발생하고 있으며, SKU를 늘려가고 있다. 
 일본의 돈키호테/로프트/마츠모토키요시는 올해 4월에 진출했다. 카테고리로는 마데카크림을 중심으로 아마존 
프라임데이에서 선전한 PDRN 아이크림을 다음 히어로 제품으로 마케팅하고 있다. 한편, 동국생명과학 진단장비 판매 
부진이 연결 기준 실적 저하의 주요인으로 작용하고 있으며, 리봄화장품은 화장품 ODM 시장 호황 효과를 톡톡히 누리고 
있다. 리봄화장품 매출의 30%만 동국제약 제품으로 성장 여력이 크다.

I. 화장품

2) 펌텍코리아: 하반기 실적 시즌 다크호스 
 3분기 펌텍코리아의 실적은 펌프와 튜브 사업 모두 QoQ 매출과 영업이익이 증가하는 호실적이 예상된다. i) 역대 최대 
화장품 글로벌 수출 모멘텀으로 용기 공급이 부족한 상황, ii) 4공장 캐파 증설 효과가 본격화되고, iii) 가격 인상 효과까지 
더해지면서 계절성이 무색해지고 있다. 튜브 부문은 5월부터 가격 인상이 실적에 반영되고 있고, 펌프 부문도 6월부터 
카테고리별로 다양하게 인상률을 책정, 반영하고 있다. 영업이익률은 19.1%(YoY +3.4%p)까지 상승할 것으로 예상한다. 
국제 유가 상승과 초과근무 증가로 원가율 상승은 부담되지만, 판관비 증가가 제한적인 상황, 매출 증가에 따른 
영업레버리지 효과가 더 클 것으로 보인다. 환율 관련 특별한 이슈는 없다. 
 4분기에는 6공장 가동 효과가 본격화된다. 1층을 사출 라인으로 채우면서 아웃소싱을 내재화했다. 그만큼 사출 캐파 여력을 
확보했을 뿐 아니라 비용 부담도 줄였다. 전년도 말부터 이어진 사출 캐파 부족 갈증을 완전히 풀게 되었다. 아울러, 
카테고리 측면에서도 4분기에는 고마진 콤팩트 매출이 증가하면서 실적 기여도를 높일 것으로 기대한다. 국내 전체적으로 
튜브 용기가 부족한 상황이다. 달바글로벌 같은 대형 인디 브랜드부터 한국콜마/코스메카코리아 같은 톱 ODM사까지 튜브 
용기 부족으로 매출이 이연된 물량들이 적지 않다. 펌프 용기와 달리 열에 훨씬 민감하기 때문에 중국산으로 대체하기도 
힘들다. 최근 국내 대형 브랜드사가 중국산 튜브 용기를 사용하려다 품질 이슈로 국내 제조사로 다시 회귀했다. 
부국티엔씨도 캐파 증설을 이어가고 있다. 전년도 디자인캐파를 증축하고 기계 설비를 계속 채워넣고 있는데, 거의 마무리 
단계에 있다. 전년 대비 30% 생산 캐파가 늘어나는 것이다. 4분기 매출과 영업이익은 전년 동기대비 69%, 170% 증가한 
1,304억원과 225억원까지 증가하면서 캐파 증설 효과를 확대할 것으로 기대한다. 부국티엔씨 4분기 매출은 330억원까지 
늘어날 것으로 예상한다.

3) 코스맥스: 계절성을 깨트리다 
 3분기 연결 매출과 영업이익은 각각 전년 동기대비 41%, 85% 증가한 8,238억원과 790억원 수준이 가능할 전망이다. 시장 
기대치(OP 677억원)를 크게 넘어서는 호실적이다. 국내 본사 매출이 YoY 45%, 중국과 미국 법인 매출이 각각 25%, 70% 
성장하며 실적 개선을 견인할 전망이다. 동남아 법인은 턴어라운드(매출 YoY 29%)가 본격화되고 있다. 특히, 국내 본사는 
계절성을 탈피, 2분기보다 높은 매출 규모가 예상되고 있다. 인디 브랜드 수출이 워낙 좋고, 글로벌 MNC와 해외 인디 
브랜드 기여도가 높아지고 있다. 기초 비중 상승과 색조 카테고리 회복, 겔마스크 효율성 제고로 국내 영업이익은 YoY 
78% 증가, 분기 최초로 600억원을 돌파할 것으로 예상한다. 
 미국에서는 현지 인디/레거시 고객사로부터 견조한 리오더 물량이 이어지는 가운데 신규 수주도 크게 증가하고 있다. 특히, 
인디 브랜드 매출 기여도가 높아지고 있으며, 토너패드/아이패치/하이드로겔마스크가 인기다. 중국 상해 법인은 현지 대형 
고객사 중심으로 YoY 30%, 광저우는 오프라인 및 수출 채널 호조로 YoY 20% 이상 외형 성장이 기대된다. 인도네시아의 
불안정한 외부환경 하에서도 YoY 30% 이상 매출 성장이 예상되며, 태국(YoY 25% 이상)도 상위 고객사에 더해 인근 
국가에서 신규 고객사 유입이 증가하고 있다.

하나증권•5

2. 수출 지표와 유사한 흐름을 보이고 있는 ODM 실적 
 최근 ODM 톱2 국내 사업 매출은 수출 실적과 유사한 흐름을 보이고 있다. 수출 실적을 보면 2분기 QoQ 24%, 3 분기 
QoQ 7% 증가했다(HS 코드 3304 기준). 코스맥스의 2분기 국내 사업 매출은 QoQ 23%, 3분기 QoQ 7% 증가할 것으로 
예상하고 있다. 한국콜마 또한 2분기 국내 사업 매출은 QoQ 25% 증가했는데, 회사에서 제시한 3분기 국내 사업 
가이던스는 QoQ 0% 성장에 불과하다. 물론, 튜브 용기 수급 이슈와 생산 캐파의 제약으로 QoQ 증가율은 제한적인 
수준에 머물 수 있지만, 그렇게 큰 차이가 날 것으로 보지 않는다. 당사에서는 3분기 QoQ 2% 증가로 추산하고 있다. 
 여기서 중요한 점은 톱2 ODM 업체들의 매출과 수출 동행성이 높아졌다는 사실이다. 2025년까지만 하더라도 QoQ는 물론 
YoY 성장률도 괴리가 있었다. 그래서, 수출 지표로 ODM 업체들의 실적을 추정한다는 것은 무리가 있었다. 최근 동행성이 
높아진 이유는 두 회사의 국내 사업에서 수출 비중이 커졌기 때문이다. 주지하다시피 국내 화장품 산업은 성장이 정체돼 
있다. 다시말해 2021년 이후 이들 기업의 국내 사업 매출 증가분은 거의 수출이라고 봐도 무방하다. 코스맥스의 경우 
2020년 전까지 국내 매출의 20% 정도만이 수출이었다. 수출이 증가해도 국내 시장이 좋지 않으면 매출 성장률이 잘 나올 
수 없는 구조였다. 현재 국내 사업 매출의 70%가 실질적으로 수출일 것으로 추정한다. 2026년 매출 증가의 대부분이 
수출이었음을 두말할 것도 없다.

 자료: 에프앤가이드, QuantiWise, 하나증권

1. 유통 3분기 실적 전망(3): 호텔신라 
1) 호텔신라: 환율 영향 크지 않을 듯 
 3분기 연결 매출은 9,688억원(YoY -5%), 영업이익은 565억원(YoY 391%, OPM 5.8%) 로 추정한다. 시장 기대치(OP 
620억원)에는 다소 못미칠 것으로 예상하는데, 주된 요인은 환율 때문이다. 다만, 환율 영향이 예상보다 크지는 않을 
것으로 보이는데, 과거와 달리 i) 다이고 주문이 들어오면 발주하기 때문에 발주시기와 매출 시기가 차이가 크지 않고, ii) 
재고 규모도 약 5천 억원으로 2~3년 전보다 천억원 이상 줄어든 상태이기 때문이다. 
 면세점 부문은 화장품 비수기로 매출과 영업이익이 모두 전분기 대비 낮아질 것으로 보인다. 시내 면세점 영향이다. 매출의 
80%가 중국인 다이고인데, 3 분기는 다이고 비수기이기 때문이다. YoY 로 보면, 인천공항 DF2 철수 때문에 매출은 
감소하고, 영업이익은 증가하는 모습이다. 면세점에서 FIT 비중은 상승, 다이고 비중은 낮아지고 있다. 단기 환율 하락은 
원가 상승에 부담이지만 할인율이 낮아지는 것은 긍정적이다. 할인률은 2분기 30% 중후반에서 소폭 낮아질 것으로 
보인다. 시내점 매출은 대부분 화장품인데, 해외 브랜드 비중이 85%로 월등히 높다. 참고로, 전년도 연말 면세점 체화재고 
때문에 4분기 손실 규모가 컸는데, 올해는 큰 영향이 없을 듯하다. 
 인천공항 DF3점은 QoQ 유사한 매출 규모를 예상한다. 전년도 8월 샤넬 매장이 그랜드 오픈했고, 외국인 인바운드 
증가하면서 패션 카테고리가 크게 성장했다. Q와 P가 모두 상승하고 있다. 인천공항점에서 외국인 매출 비중이 조금씩 
늘고 있다. 내국인 매출 비중은 30%, 외국인 매출 가운데 중국인이 절반이 조금 넘는다. 한편, DF3 임차료 부담은 조금 
커졌다. 임차료는 객수 기준인데, 방학으로 학생 여행객 비중이 늘면서 면세점 매출 증가폭은 상대적으로 제한적이었기 
때문이다. 해외공항점 임차료 사정도 마찬가지다. 
 호텔 사업 매출은 2분기 대비 소폭 증가할 것으로 보인다. 서울점과 스테이는 2분기와 유사하고, 제주점은 성수기 효과로 
QoQ 증가하는 구조다. 호텔 매출은 ADR 상승 효과가 크다. 서울점은 YoY 18%, 스테이는 15%, 제주점도 10% 이상 
증가한 것으로 추산한다. 투숙률은 전년도 3분기와 유사한 수준으로, 서울점은 75%, 제주와 스테이는 80~85%로 
추정한다. 다만, 서울점은 식음/연회 비중이 50%가 넘기 때문에 ADR 상승만큼 매출이 증가한다고 보기 어렵다.

2. 9월 외국인 관광소비 소폭 둔화 
 8월 외국인 인바운드 관광객은 YoY 23% 증가한 225만명을 기록했다. 증가율만 보면 지난 3월(YoY 27%) 이후 가장 
높았고, 규모로 보면 월간 기준 역대 최대 수치다. 중국인 증가율이 가장 높았다(83만명, YoY 37%). 9 월 외국인 관광 
소비는 YoY 21% 증가한 1.83조원을 기록했다. 지금 추세로 가면 국내 소비 시장의 2% 비중까지 이르게 될 것으로 
보인다. 다만, 증가율은 둔화되었다. 2분기 YoY 32%에 달했던 관광소비 증가율이 3분기에는 YoY 25%로 떨어졌다. 백화점 
업체들 실적과 연관이 큰 대형쇼핑몰 관광소비 증가율 역시 3분기 YoY 29%로 2분기(YoY 46%)에 비해 하락했다. 환율 
하락에 따른 외국인 인바운드 둔화에 의한 것인지, 환율 하락에 따른 원화 계상 매출이 줄어든 것인지는 지켜봐야 한다. 
한편, 외국인 관광소비 가운데, 약국 채널과 뷰티 카테고리는 각각 YoY 142%, 92% 높은 신장세를 지속하고 있다.

 영국 부츠, 캐나다 투자회사가 89억 달러 조건 인수(뷰티누리) 
- 캐나다 위팅턴 인베스트먼트가 영국 뷰티·헬스 체인 '부츠'를 시카모어 파트너스로부터 89억 달러에 인수하기로 합의, 1년 8개월 만에 재매각됨 
- 위팅턴을 소유한 웨스턴 가문은 뷰티·유통 분야 전문성을 바탕으로 매장 업그레이드와 온라인·헬스케어 서비스 확대에 투자할 계획이며, 절차는 내년 
1분기 중 마무리될 전망임 
URL: https://buly.kr/Chrodmo

 화장품으로 뚫고 ‘기기’로 넓힌다…美서 보폭 넓히는 뷰티테크(쿠키뉴스) 
- K뷰티가 미국 시장에서 화장품을 넘어 홈 뷰티 디바이스로 영역을 넓히는 가운데, 앳홈의 '톰'이 세포라 580여개 매장에 입점하고 에이피알의 'AGE-R'도 
올 상반기 약 2446억원 매출을 올림 
- 글로벌 홈 뷰티 디바이스 시장이 2030년 45조원까지 성장할 전망인 가운데, 화장품으로 쌓은 K뷰티 인지도가 디바이스 시장 진입을 뒷받침하며 
온·오프라인 접점도 확대되고 있음 
URL: https://buly.kr/EoquPbs

 "글로벌 색조 화장품 공략…내년 상반기 100만개 수출"(파이낸셜뉴스) 
- 피엔케이피부임상연구센타 자회사 케이오니리카가 선제적 투자와 색조 개발 역량을 바탕으로 매출 회수 구간에 진입, 내년 상반기 해외에 색조 화장품 
100만개 이상 공급을 예정함 
- 지난해 매출이 전년比 3.5배 성장하고 해외 매출 비중도 2배 늘어난 가운데, 세포라 입점 미국 브랜드와 유럽 브랜드 신제품 개발을 수주해 리오더 
연결을 목표로 함 
URL: https://buly.kr/C0Cmiah

 로레알 인수 K-스킨케어 브랜드 ‘틱톡 샵’ 입점(뷰티누리) 
- 로레알 그룹이 인수한 K-스킨케어 브랜드 '닥터지'가 10월 1일부터 미국 '틱톡 샵'에 공식 입점하며 블랙 스네일 컬렉션 등 베스트셀러 7종을 선보임 
- 아마존 데뷔에 이은 이번 입점으로 미국 내 브랜드 인지도를 끌어올리고, 크리에이터 파트너십과 뉴욕·LA 이벤트 등 전방위 마케팅을 전개할 계획임 
URL: https://buly.kr/5qAEw1U

 “강남·잠실점 새단장"…롯데백화점, 상권 초격차 전략 본격화(이데일리) 
- 롯데백화점이 강남점·잠실점 리뉴얼에 나서며 강남점은 프리미엄 하이퍼 로컬 점포로, 잠실점은 식품관 개편을 시작으로 럭셔리관까지 확대 개편함 
- 앞서 리뉴얼을 마친 인천점·노원점이 각각 매출 20%, 15% 성장한 성과를 바탕으로, 국내 최다 점포(30개)를 보유한 상권 초격차 전략을 본격화하고 있음 
URL: https://buly.kr/EoquPnF

- 최근 1년간 신선식품 구매액에서 과일류·채소류가 1·2위를 차지해 육류보다 많았으며, 대형할인점 비중은 여전히 가장 높지만 1%포인트 하락함 
- 편의점 신선식품 구매액이 전년比 20.4% 급증하고 온라인 구매도 늘어난 반면 오프라인 구매는 감소해, 신선식품 구매 채널이 세분화되는 흐름이 
나타남 
URL: https://buly.kr/6teoH1g

- 현대백화점이 '더현대 글로벌' 플랫폼으로 프랑스 갤러리 라파예트 파리 오스만점에서 K패션 5개 브랜드와 팝업스토어를 열며 유럽 진출을 본격화함 
- 신세계백화점도 뉴욕 허드슨야드에서 첫 미국 팝업스토어를 열어 30여 개 K뷰티 브랜드를 선보이며, 정부·공공기관 지원하에 K백화점의 해외 진출이

Compliance Notice  투자등급 관련사항 및 투자의견 비율공시

 당사는 2026년 10월 13일 현재 해당회사의 지분을 1%이상 보유 하고 있지 않습니다  
 본 자료를 작성한 애널리스트(박종대)는 자료의 작성과 관련하여 외부의 압력이나 부당
한 간섭을 받지 않았으며, 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 신의성실 하게 작성하였
습니다. 
 본 자료는 기관투자가 등 제 3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
 본 자료를 작성한 애널리스트(박종대)는 2026년 10월 13일 현재 해당회사의 유가증권
을 보유하고 있지 않습니다

 투자의견의 유효기간은 추천일 이후 12개월을 기준으로 적용 
 기업의 분류 
BUY(매수)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 15% 이상 상승 여력 
Neutral(중립)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 -15%~15% 등락 
Reduce(비중축소)_목표주가가 현주가 대비 -15% 이상 하락 가능 
 산업의 분류 
Overweight(비중확대)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 15% 이상 상승 여력 
Neutral(중립)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 -15%~15% 등락 
Underweight(비중축소)_업종지수가 현재지수 대비 -15% 이상 하락 가능 
투자등급 BUY(매수) Neutral(중립) Reduce(매도) 합계 
금융투자상품의 비율 98.18% 1.82% 0.00% 100% 
* 기준일: 2026년 10월 10일

본 조사자료는 고객의 투자에 정보를 제공할 목적으로 작성되었으며, 어떠한 경우에도 
무단 복제 및 배포 될 수 없습니다. 또한 본 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사가 신뢰할 만한 
자료 및 정보로 얻어진 것이나, 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없으므로 투자자 자
신의 판단과 책임하에 최종결정을 하시기 바랍니다. 따라서 어떠한 경우에도 본 자료는 
고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재의 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다.

## Document 595: [產業] 철강 Weekly - 철근, 미국 수출특수가 지나간 후 ... - 유진투자증권

Source PDF: all report pdf/[產業] 철강 Weekly - 철근, 미국 수출특수가 지나간 후 ... - 유진투자증권.pdf
Report source: Unknown

### Page 1

Weekly | 철강금속 | 2026.10.07

철강 김소정_ 02)368-6163_ smalljung@eugenefn.com

수요 공백으로 3 분기 제강사 마진 하락 불가피 
상반기 철근 수출은 58 만톤을 기록한 반면, 3 분기 철근 수출은 11 만톤에 그치
며 월평균 수출량이 상반기 대비 62% 감소하였다. 이는 6 월 이후 원화강세에 
따른 수출 채산성 악화와 미국 반덤핑 제소 우려로 주요 제강사가 미국향 수출
을 중단한 영향이다. 호주 등 대체 시장에서도 미국만큼의 물량 확보가 어려워 
단기간 내 수출 회복은 제한적일 전망이다. 
국내 주택 건설경기 부진에 따른 철근 수요의 공백도 여전하다. 특히, 3 분기
(7~9 월)는 계절적 건설 비수기로 철근 유통 수요가 급감하였고 그에 따라 유통
가격은 6 월 88.5 만원에서 85 만원까지 하향조정 되었다. 
철근 수출 정점을 기록한 5 월(13 만톤) 이래로 8 대 제강사의 재고는 증가 추세
에 있다. 극한 수요 침체 시황에서 주요 제강사는 재고 수준을 낮추고자 공급조
절에 나섰다. 현대제철·동국제강·대한제강·와이케이스틸·한국특강은 추석 연휴와 
연계해 장기 휴동에 들어갔으며, 이 중 현대제철과 한국특강은 대보수까지 병행
한 것으로 파악된다. 
감산은 재고 부담을 덜어주지만, 가동률 하락에 따라 3분기 제강사 마진 축소는 
불가피하다. 수요가 견조한 H 형강을 함께 생산하는 현대제철과 동국제강은 철
근 부진을 일부 만회하겠으나 실적 눈높이는 하향조정이 필요하다.

Key Chart: 미국향 수출 급감하자 제강사 롤마진 축소

주1: 철근 출하량은 내수판매와 수출의 합계 
주2: 출하량과 수출량은 10월 추정치까지 표기하였음 
자료: 업계자료, 유진투자증권 
자료: 스틸데일리, 유진투자증권

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 
따라서 어떠한 경우에도 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

투자기간 및 투자등급/투자의견 비율 
종목추천 및 업종추천 투자기간: 12개월  (추천기준일 종가대비 추천종목의 예상 목표수익률을 의미함) 당사 투자의견 비율(%) 
ㆍSTRONG BUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +50%이상 3% 
ㆍBUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +15%이상 ~ +50%미만 94% 
ㆍHOLD(중립) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%이상 ∼ +15%미만 3% 
ㆍREDUCE(매도) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%미만 0% 
(2026.09.30 기준)

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Central banks’ communication is carefully scrutinised for
every nuance. Lately, there seems to have been more RBA
communication to pore over. The method of delivery has
also evolved. This has sometimes caused confusion when
tone seems to vary. Different audiences call for different

tone and varying complexity.
• The content has evolved, too. RBA officials are increasingly
using speeches directed to professional audiences to
discuss more technical estimates of inferred variables such
as neutral rates and financial conditions. They are also
highlighting that monetary policy combats inflation in more
ways than just squeezing mortgage holders.
• One thing has not changed: the RBA is not in the business
of providing ‘forward guidance’ promises about the future

path of interest rates. It does, however, give more running
commentary about how it is interpreting recent data. These
interpretations are themselves useful signals about what
the RBA thinks it might do.
Market participants pore over every utterance by central banks
for clues to future rate moves. They will have plenty to pore over
in the run-up to the November RBA decision. After the flurry
of fireside chats this week, ’60 Minutes’ will run a pre-recorded

interview with chief economist Sarah Hunter on Sunday. Next
Wednesday, the whole senior policy team will appear before
Senate Estimates, just as the quarterly CPI is released. (This
timing will have been the Senate’s choice, not the RBA’s.) After
the decision will come the Statement on Monetary Policy, a
media release and press conference. More speeches will follow,
including one by the Deputy Governor on the Thursday.
RBA communication does seem to have changed, not just

following the RBA Review but also more recently. There may
be fewer policy meetings each year, but the communication
outside that certainly feels more frequent than a couple of years
ago. Back in 2024, speaking engagements felt a little sparse
outside the post-meeting media conference and parliamentary
appearances. We have counted 58 public engagements so
far this year on the Speeches page on the RBA’s website.
This compares with 58 for the whole of last year and 52 in

2024. Excluding the ‘compulsory’ press conferences and
parliamentary appearances, fewer than half of the 2024 events
were related to economics, markets and monetary policy.
The ‘how’ has changed
The way the RBA communicates with markets has changed
as well. There are fewer set-piece speeches on policy and a
Open mouth operations
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
lot more semi-scripted ‘fireside chats’. Compared with a few
years ago, there are more interviews across a wide range of

media. This is a big change from the pre-Review world. It was
a huge deal when the previous Governor appeared on the
7:30 Report ; it is hard to imagine him agreeing to an Assistant
Governor appearing on 60 Minutes.
Part of the change reflects increased efforts to reach a general
audience. Central banks want to explain why they do what
they do. This matters especially when interest rates are rising
but the household sector is not the source of the inflation.

Speaking differently to different audiences can sometimes
confuse markets. As the RBA’s Chief Communication Officer
highlighted in a recent panel discussion, the post-meeting
media conference is designed to speak to the public. Market
participants and economists certainly tune in, but we are not
the primary audience. So, if you think that what the Governor
said in the media conference sounded more ‘dovish’ than
what another official says in a more market-oriented forum,

it is partly because she is trying to convey that the RBA
understands that its recent policy actions are making things
harder for many Australians. But this is in much the same way
that a doctor might say, “This might hurt a bit.” It does, but that
will not stop the treatment.
A related issue is that it is easier to control tone in pre-drafted
material such as the minutes and set-piece speeches than in
unstructured Q&A. Senior central bankers are trained to be

careful with their words in unscripted settings. If the tone
differs, the minutes are still more likely to say what the Board
really means than an off-the-cuff answer.
Greater communication with the broader public might have
reduced the pressure to make the set-piece speeches more
accessible. Research on central bank communication often
uses measures such as the Flesch–Kincaid Grade Level and
Flesch Reading Ease score. A comprehensive analysis was

beyond the scope of this note. However, a spot comparison
suggests four speeches from 2026 ( 1, 2, 3, 4) were harder
to read than four from 2021 (1, 2, 3, 4). The 2021 speeches
scored in the 11 to 12 range, which is high-school-level
“The RBA does not want to promise what it will
do, but it is increasingly trying to tell us what
it thinks. "

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
complexity. The 2026 speeches scored between 12 and
15, which is academic or technically dense text requiring
university-level education. (Including two of my own 2021
speeches is an obvious caveat, but the two speeches by then-
Governor Phil Lowe also scored better on these metrics than

the sample from this year. It should be noted, though, that
readability metrics do not necessarily imply better clarity if the
concepts discussed are more abstract.)
The ‘what’ has changed
As noted above, the content of RBA communication has
evolved, too. As its intellectual framework has begun to put
more weight on the balance of supply and demand, and those
invisible ‘star’ variables (NAIRUs, neutral real rates, ‘financial
conditions’, etc.), the communication to professional audiences

has increasingly focused on those topics.
More recently, the RBA has also placed more emphasis on
the full range of transmission channels from interest rates to
inflation (though not, as we have noted previously, the labour
supply channel). This is understandable in a world of supply
shocks. People need to understand that monetary policy is not
simply an exercise in hitting mortgage borrowers. We doubt
they would be comforted, though, by the Deputy Governor’s

explanation this week that the ‘cashflow channel’ was the
smallest of the transmission channels from interest rates
to inflation, with the exchange rate much more prominent.
That implies a lot of pain for a small group, for little effect on
inflation. It raises the question of whether other policy tools
or institutional arrangements might get the same effect on
inflation through the other channels without hitting a minority
of households disproportionately.

We have also seen a move away from using catchphrases
to summarise the position. From ‘narrow path’ to ‘not
ruling anything in or out’ to ‘cautious and predictable’, once
entrenched, a phrase can outlast its usefulness.
One thing that has not changed is the way the minutes record
the presentations to the Board and the members’ discussion. The
minutes rarely convey much new information beyond the post-
meeting statements. Sometimes, though, the minutes reveal the

Board’s reaction to the staff’s analysis. The August 2024 minutes
were a good example: the discussion highlighted just how
sensitive the forecasts were to key judgements by the staff.
The ‘why’ has not changed
Despite these changes, the underlying objectives of the RBA’s
communication have not changed. It wants to explain – and
perhaps justify – its decisions. And it wants to describe how
it interprets incoming data, especially inflation, the labour

market and the balance of supply and demand.
The RBA, like any central bank, is not really in the business
of providing ‘forward guidance’ about future moves.
Circumstances change, so it can never guarantee a particular
future path for interest rates. Some academic literature argues
that promising to keep rates low for a long time (or high,
for that matter) independently amplifies the transmission
of monetary policy beyond the effect of current-period

interest rates alone, by shifting the short end of the yield
curve. Central banks including the RBA have certainly tried
jawboning in this way from time to time. The prevailing view,
though, is that the potential cost of reputational loss if things
do not turn out as predicted outweighs the benefits of a
slightly stronger transmission mechanism.
In this context, it is the interpretation of data that provides
the signal about future policy directions. This is why it was

noteworthy that the Governor still characterised the labour
market as ‘a little tight’ after the September meeting, even
though the Board’s post-meeting communication did not. We
have been noting for some time that the RBA staff have a
downbeat view of supply capacity. Together with the RBA’s
oft-stated concerns about inflation, this is why the Deputy
Governor’s ‘hawkishness’ did not surprise us.

THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: managing expectations
While annual retail sales growth improved in September,
rising from 0.4%yr to 1.1%yr, the current pace of growth
is only a quarter of the gain seen just a year ago and very
weak versus history. Consumer spending continues to be

constrained by weak confidence, particularly with respect to
employment prospects and wealth. On housing wealth, five
years into the property downturn, house prices continue to
decline month-on-month and investment remains in a deep
downturn, –20.6%ytd.
Total fixed asset investment has been detracting from
annual GDP growth since April owing to a lull in high-tech
investment, a lack of confidence in future demand amongst
domestically oriented businesses as well as property’s

malaise. For aggregate growth to hold up through 2027 and
2028 as the contribution from net exports moderates, broad-
based stimulus in scale will prove necessary. Expect recent
targeted support measures to be broadened and given much
greater scale in coming months.
Finally, in the UK, inflation accelerated from 3.1%yr to 3.5%yr
in September, exceeding both market expectations and the
Bank of England's July forecast. The surprise was largely
driven by energy, fuel prices rising 5.4%mth. However, core

inflation also edged up from 2.6%yr to 2.8%yr as services
inflation increased from 3.4%yr to 3.7%yr. While not an
immediate concern inflation, the labour market showing
resilience, the ILO unemployment rate falling from 4.9% to
4.7% in August, suggests demand risks for inflation will need
close monitoring as well as supply-side concerns.
Next week will see the focus once again return to the US as
the FOMC meet and US Q3 GDP is released. For both events,

the implications for risks to the outlook will be the focus. The
week after, the US mid-term elections are due.
With little domestic data of significance, participants’
attention focused on RBA Deputy Governor Hauser's fireside
chat, which saw an abrupt shift in market expectations of a
follow-up rate hike at the November meeting.
Hauser warned in his remarks that the current inflation outlook
was "materially worse" than the RBA's August forecasts.

Three upside inflation risks were characterised as having
"crystallised" ahead of the September meeting – the Middle
East, the AI boom, and domestic supply constraints. Hauser
noted the Middle East picture "looks worse" given oil prices
are "about a third higher than [the RBA] assumed in August”.
He then positioned AI and data centre developments among
an "extraordinary sequence of positive demand shocks around
the world", with "higher AI prices, tech prices feeding through

into CPI". Finally, on domestic capacity pressures, the Deputy
Governor noted that "inflation's been coming in at or a little
bit above expectations, despite an apparent easing in the
labour market". He went on to state that the staff are currently
assessing "whether [the RBA] need to change [its] judgement
on capacity pressures in the labour market again" – in other
words, revise up their assumption for the NAIRU.
Hauser's comments prompted a material reassessment of

market pricing for a follow-up November hike, from just a
20% chance at the end of last week to a 50/50 proposition
today ahead of next week's Q3 CPI. For that release, we are
forecasting a 1.3% (4.1%yr) lift in headline inflation and a
0.9 (3.5%yr) increase in trimmed mean inflation. Our preview
provides more detail behind the component mix for Q3 as
well as our upward revisions to the preliminary Q4 forecast.
Provided inflation prints broadly in line with our expectations,

the RBA is likely to hike by 25bp at the November meeting.
Across in New Zealand, consumer prices rose 0.7% in Q3
and annual inflation eased from 4.1%yr to 3.7%yr. Under
the surface of this ‘as expected’ read though, several closely
watched measures of core inflation took a step higher to now
be at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s target band. Our
NZ economics team consequently expect the RBNZ will raise
the OCR 25bps to 3.00% at its October policy review, while

retaining a 4.00% terminal OCR forecast for September 2027.
Earlier in the week, China's economy surprised  to the upside in
Q3, GDP growth accelerating from 0.9%qtr to 1.2%qtr, lifting
annual growth from 4.3%yr to 4.5%yr – keeping authorities on
track to achieve their 4.5–5.0% full-year growth objective.
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
RBNZ now expected to hike in October
We now expect the RBNZ will deliver a 25bp hike at its
policy review on 28 October. While September quarter
inflation was slightly below the RBNZ’s forecast, the
underlying detail was firm. More recently, increases in

fuel prices point to a material lift in inflation by year-end.
We expect further interest rate increases over the coming
months, with the OCR reaching a peak of 4% next year.
Softer headline inflation but firm core pressures
leave RBNZ on course to hike in October
We now expect the RBNZ to raise the Official Cash Rate
by 25bp at its October policy review (previously, we had
expected a pause at this meeting). This change in view reflects
significant upside risks to near-term inflation relative to the

RBNZ’s previous forecast.
While in line with our forecast, the September quarter inflation
figures were slightly softer than the RBNZ expected, with prices
rising 3.7% over the past year (compared with the RBNZ’s
forecast of 3.9%). However, the details were less reassuring.
Excluding volatile items such as food and fuel, underlying
inflation pressures remained firm. Closely watched measures
of core inflation moved higher and now sit close to, or a little

above, the top of the RBNZ’s target band. We also saw an
increase in the number of goods recording price rises, taking
that measure back to levels seen in the wake of the pandemic.
Alongside the existing firmness in underlying inflation, the
risks for inflation over the coming quarters are firmly tilted to
the upside. That near-term upside risk has also been noted by
the Governor. Following a sharp rise in global fuel costs, we
expect inflation to reach 4.3% by the end of this year (above

the RBNZ’s September MPS forecast for +3.9%). While that is
mainly due to higher prices at the pump, businesses in sectors
such as construction are also reporting related increases in
transport and materials costs. Consistent with those trends,
we expect core inflation to remain in the upper part of the
RBNZ’s target band through 2027.
Reinforcing the case for an OCR hike at the October meeting,
the New Zealand dollar is notably weaker than the RBNZ

assumed: on a trade-weighted basis, the NZD is currently
3.6% below the RBNZ’s September projections. If sustained,
that would add to inflation pressures over the next few
quarters. We suspect this will not have shifted the RBNZ’s
medium-term outlook for inflation.
Several other developments need to be weighed against that
firmer inflation outlook. First, wholesale long-term interest rates
have risen markedly, and there has also been a more moderate

rise in some mortgage rates. The RBNZ is likely to note the role of
those increases in managing medium-term inflation risks.
In addition, the RBNZ is likely to continue to emphasise that
there is little evidence of a lift in businesses’ longer-term plans
for price setting at this stage in response to the rise in energy
prices (sometimes referred to as “second-round inflation
effects”). We expect the RBNZ to highlight the risk that more
persistent inflation pressures could emerge, depending on the

duration of the oil price shock.
We also expect the RBNZ to note that the labour market
remains cyclically weak and is not a significant driver of inflation.
Employment growth that remains weak relative to labour force
growth, subdued growth in job vacancies and low labour market
turnover are consistent with this view and could be emphasised.
Finally, the RBNZ is likely to acknowledge that while the
global growth outlook remains solid, there are significant

downside risks from the Iran war and concerns about the
sustainability of the AI investment cycle.
Balancing those concerns, we think the MPC will lift the
cash rate next week. However, a vote seems very possible as
differences in view will likely remain among the MPC about
how much the medium-term inflation outlook has really
shifted in recent weeks.
In September, the bulk of the MPC saw upside risks to the
inflation outlook while two members (Conway and Hansen)

thought the risks were balanced. Recent developments will
not have made those seeing upside risks to inflation more
Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
comfortable, while the more dovish MPC members will
likely focus on the ongoing downside risks for growth given
continued softness in house prices and the mounting pressure
on real incomes. Indeed, all MPC members saw some degree
of downside risks to growth which likely remain relevant. All

these messages should remain in the RBNZ’s updated views.
The MPC is likely to continue to present its decision as a
gradual evolution of the approach taken since July: a higher
OCR now provides insurance against the need for a much
higher OCR later if upside risks to medium-term inflation
materialise. We doubt the aim will be to push market interest
rates materially higher.
RBNZ to leave door open for further hikes, OCR
still expected to peak at 4% in 2027

In its forward guidance, the RBNZ is likely to leave open the option
of raising the OCR further at the December Monetary Policy
Statement, without presenting an increase as certain. While the
RBNZ will not publish an updated OCR projection at this review,
the press statement is likely to note that further increases may
be needed, depending on economic conditions. The September
projection showed the OCR eventually rising to 3.25%.
We expect the RBNZ will end up delivering another 25bp

hike in December, followed by a further hike in February.
That would take the OCR to 3.5%, roughly where the RBNZ
estimates the neutral rate to be. At that point, we expect the
RBNZ to slow the pace of rate increases (previously, we had
expected that point to be reached a little later).
We continue to expect further 25bp hikes at the June and
September 2027 meetings, taking the OCR to a peak of 4.0%.
While oil prices are generating significant swings in inflation,

underlying pressures remain firm. More restrictive conditions are
likely to be needed to bring core inflation sustainably back to 2%.
Scenarios
Our central scenario, with a probability of around 60%, is that
the RBNZ increases the OCR by 25bp and signals that a further
increase in December remains an option, without committing to
a markedly tighter future stance than it outlined in September.
Alternative scenarios include:
• A hawkish scenario (around a 20% probability) would see

the OCR increase to 3%, with the RBNZ placing greater
emphasis on upside risks to core and medium-term inflation.
That would suggest an upward revision to the RBNZ’s
projected OCR path in December. Downside risks to the
exchange rate could also be emphasised.
• A dovish scenario (around a 20% probability) would
see the RBNZ leave the OCR unchanged and signal that
the medium-term inflation outlook has not changed
meaningfully from its September forecast, despite higher

near-term inflation. In this scenario, the RBNZ would
reiterate its previous forecast: a 25bp increase in December
and one further hike during 2027.
How might the election play into the decision?
While next week’s meeting is close to the General Election
on 7 November, the decision taken by the MPC will not be
politically motivated. However, the election outcome is close.
And should it result in a Labour Party-led coalition, it seems
likely the new government would adopt the Labour Party’s

promise to restore the RBNZ’s dual mandate. That would
require the MPC to weigh the degree of labour market slack
more highly in its deliberations. As the degree of labour market
slack currently seems high, a new mandate might justify the
MPC taking more time to return inflation to the middle of the
target range (our earlier note discusses this issue). This could
figure into the deliberations of some MPC members if the
decision between leaving the OCR unchanged and raising it

now is viewed as finely balanced.

AUS: Sep Private Sector Credit (%mth)
Oct 30, Last: 0.6, Westpac f/c: 0.5
Market f/c: 0.5, Range: 0.4 to 0.6
Private sector credit growth came in slightly firmer than
expected at 0.6%mth in August, above Westpac’s and the
market’s forecast for a 0.5%mth gain, leaving credit growth

running at 8.4%yr. Growth in housing credit continued to ease,
as we expected, though the mix highlights that the waning in
momentum is uneven, centred on investors rather than owner-
occupiers. Business credit meanwhile remains a source of
strength, notwithstanding some slowing recently.
For September, we expect credit growth to nudge to 0.5%mth,
with the divergence between housing credit and business
credit persisting.
AUS: Q3/Sep CPI (%qtr/%mth)

Oct 28, Quarter, Last: 0.6%, Westpac f/c: 1.3%,
Market f/c: 1.3%, Range: 0.6 to 1.4.
Oct 28, Month, Last: 0.4%, Westpac f/c: 0.6%,
Market f/c: 0.6 Range: 0.3 to 0.8
As noted in the review of the August CPI, our September
quarter CPI estimate is unchanged at 1.3%qtr holding the
annual pace flat at 3.9%yr. At the margin there was a 0.04ppt
increase due to a lift in auto fuel but it still rounds to 1.3%.
The seasonally adjusted CPI gains 1.1% highlighting that part

of the increase is due to usual seasonality.
In the month, the September CPI is expected to rise 0.6% with
the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr. Recreation & culture make
the largest contribution but transport is not far behind. Our
seasonally adjusted estimate increases 0.5%.
For more details please see “ September CPI Preview “.

NZ: RBNZ Official Cash Rate Review (%)
Oct 28, Last: 2.75, Westpac f/c: 3.00, Mkt f/c: 3.00
We expect the RBNZ to raise the OCR by 25bps to 3% at
the 28 October Review. Data since September show a higher
outlook for year-end inflation and a stronger GDP starting

point, but increased risks to domestic consumption and
residential investment. On balance, the medium-term inflation
picture will probably be judged to have worsened slightly

NZ: Oct ANZ Business Confidence (index)
Oct 29, Last: 51.9
Firms remained optimistic in the September survey of business
confidence, with both the activity and inflation measures
broadly holding steady. However, it’s likely that most responses
were submitted in the early part of the month, while fuel prices
took a sharp step higher from the second half of September. The

survey comes too late for the RBNZ’s October policy review, but
evidence about the breadth of cost and price pressures could
have a bearing on the RBNZ’s thinking about the total amount of
policy tightening needed in the months ahead.
NZ: Sep Monthly Employment Indicator (%mth)
Oct 28, Last: 0.2, Westpac f/c: 0.3
The MEI has been gradually ticking higher in recent months,
showing that the jobs market has held its ground through
the Iran conflict and the consequent surge in fuel prices. The

weekly snapshots provided by Stats NZ have been running
ahead of year-ago levels and suggest another solid result for
September – we’ve pencilled in a 0.3% increase. Given the
history of this measure, however, both this month and the
previous month are likely to be revised down a little from their
initial release.

### Page 9

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.9
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Oct FOMC meeting
Oct 27-28, Last: 3.875%, Mkt f/c: 3.875%, WBC f/c: 3.875%
Core CPI inflation and producer price inflation both surprised
to the upside in September, and the price of Brent oil has
risen back to around USD110. Underlying momentum in
the economy also remains robust, although employment

disappointed in the latest update. Given the concerns shown
by Committee members over price risks and their collective
resolve to return annual inflation to target, the above data
could easily justify a follow-up hike at the October meeting.
That said, Committee members who have spoken recently
have shown patience with respect to assessing the economy
and positioing policy. The market has responded, effectively
pricing out the chance of an October hike in favour of a

December move, which is also our expectation.
US Q3 GDP
Oct 29, %ann’d, Last: 2.2%, Mkt f/c: 3.0%, WBC f/c: 3.1%
The US economy continues to grow at a pace well above trend.
This outperformance is almost entirely due to the strength of
investment in data centres and other AI related assets. The
pipeline for this activity remains strong, limiting near-term
risks to the downside.
Abstracting from AI investment’s contribution, other business
investment and residential investment is weak, weighed down

by high term interest rates and uncertainty related to trade
policy. Household consumption growth is volatile month-to-
month, but the trend is solid. Absent a marked deterioration
in the labour market, which is not our expectation, this
momentum is likely to persist, as wealth continues to grow and
household income gains neutralises inflation. Come 2027 and
2028, growth is expected to slow to trend, but not below.

## Document 696: Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds

Source PDF: all report pdf/Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Compelling risk-reward in 
US 10-year bonds  
Summary 
Event: The US 10 -year government bond yield has surged 
past 5.0% to a 19-year high and has now entered our three -
month forecast range of 5.0-5.25%. At the time of writing, the 
10-year yield is trading at 5.2%, its highest since June 2007’s 
high of 5.32%. The next resistance levels are the 2002 high 
of 5.47% and the 2001 high of 5.53%.  
We view the risk/reward for the US 10-year government 
bond after the recent yield spike as  compelling and we 
are initiating a long opportunistic idea at the current 
yield. As the table on the right shows, if the 10 -year yield 
rises to 6%, investors would face less than 1% in negative 
total returns over a one-year period, whereas if the yield falls 
below 5.0%, they would gain at least 6% in total returns.  
While we continue to prefer the 3-7-year bond maturity range 
for income generation (the US 5-year bond yield is also 
above 5%), and broadly prefer corporate over government 
bonds amid solid corporate fundamentals, the US 10 -year 
government bond yield has finally reached reasonable levels 
for investors looking for slightly longer maturities. 
Rationale: We expect the US 10-year government bond yield 
to ease to 4.75-5.0% over a 12-month period due to: 
Easing oil prices and inflation over next 12 months: While 
the yield is likely to remain elevated in the next three months 
due to elevated oil prices caused by geopolitical uncertainty, 
heavy AI investment-driven bond issuance and a resilient US 
economy, it is likely to ease towards the 4.75 -5.0% range 
over 12  months as oil prices moderate either due to a 
resolution in the Middle East or a rerouting of Middle East 
energy supplies and as the impact of tariffs fade.  
We expect s hallower Fed rate hikes than market s: We 
expect a total of 50bps of Fed rate hikes by June 2027, before 
a cut in H2 as US inflation eventually eases towards the Fed’s 
2% target. This  rate trajectory is shallower than c. 95bps of 
further hikes priced by markets over the next 12 months. 
 The benchmark US 10-year government bond yield has 
risen to its highest since 2007, offering medium-term 
investors a compelling risk-reward trade-off 
US 10-year government bond yield and previous highs

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered

At current levels, the US 10-year government bond yield 
offers investors compelling risk-reward trade off

year Developed Market government bonds from shifts in 
their yields; grey dotted lines roughly denote current spot 
yield levels

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Investor positioning adjustment: In the very near-term, the 
US 10 -year bond yield has room to move lower as hedge 
funds using systematic strategies (CTAs) are extremely short 
US government bonds, raising the chance of short -covering 
if upcoming jobs and inflation data miss estimates. 
A shallow Fed hiking cycle  
We expect the shallowest Fed hiking cycle in almost 
three decades. This should enable the US 10-year yield to 
ease towards 4.75 -5.0% over a 12 -month period. Following 
this month’s increase, we forecast two further 25bp hikes by 
June 2027, followed by a cut in H2 2027. Markets price at 
least three more hikes by June and a strong probability of a 
fourth by September. Our projected 75bp s of total hikes this 
cycle compares with 525bps of hikes in 2022–23, 225bps in 
2015–18, 425bps in 2004–06 and 175bp in 1999–2000. The 
last smaller mid-cycle move was a single 25bps hike in 1997. 
Why we expect a more cautious Fed 
The latest hike restored the Fed’s credibility after renewed 
oil-price pressures, elevated inflation and a balanced labour 
market prompted a unanimous policy vote. However, the 
following factors argue against aggressive tightening: 
Supply-driven inflation: Energy costs and AI-infrastructure 
constraints, rather than excess demand, are driving prices. 
Higher rates cannot repair supply and may weaken growth. 
Labour is no longer the main inflation source:  Labour 
supply and demand are better balanced, wage growth has 
moderated and unemployment is near full employment. AI 
further weakens the traditional Phillips Curve relationship 
between unemployment and inflation. 
Disinflation should resume:  Energy pressures may fade 
after the US mid-term elections, while the one-off inflationary 
effects of tariffs should diminish after 2025–26. 
AI raises the neutral rate , but dampens inflation:  
Investment and productivity may lift the neutral rate ( r*), the 
rate consistent with stable inflation and full employment, 
while efficiency gains limit wage-price spirals. 
Markets are already tightening  financial conditions : 
Higher bond yields have raised borrowing costs and 
restrained activity, doing part of the Fed’s work. 
The cycle began from a higher base:  The Fed funds rate 
started at 3.75%, versus 0.25% in the previous two cycles 
and 1% before that, reducing additional tightening required. 
Monetary transmission has weakened:  Wealthier 
households drive more spending but are less rate -sensitive, 
while lower -income consumers are already constrained. 
Further hikes may , therefore, curb demand less effectively 
while causing disproportionate harm to low-income families. 
 We expect a shallower Fed rate hiking cycle compared 
with market expectations 
Our policy rate outlook for the Fed, ECB and BoJ

tariffs, long-term inflation expectations remain subdued

Small business conditions are deteriorating:  Higher 
financing and energy costs are weakening sentiment, as 
reflected in the latest Beige Book . The renewed 
underperformance of US small capitalisation (S&P 600) and 
mid capitalisation (S&P 400) equity indices  versus the large 
cap S&P 500 index over the past couple of months also show 
rising pressure on small businesses. 
Fiscal and financial -stability risks are rising:  Aggressive 
Fed tightening would increase US debt-servicing costs as the 
US Treasury seeks cheaper funding through shorter-maturity 
issuance, while potentially amplifying financial-system stress. 
Upside risk to bond yields 
Our view of lower bond yields carries the following risks: 
Persistent inflation:  Repeated supply shocks, strong AI -
related investment demand and tighter immigration controls 
could keep inflation above target and force steeper Fed rate 
hikes. With unemployment near the Fed’s estimate of full 
employment, a sharp drop in labour supply could intensify 
wage pressures and destabilise inflation expectations . We 
note that long-term expectations remain contained for now. 
Geopolitical escalation:  An intensification of geopolitical 
conflicts after the mid -term elections could lift energy prices 
and inflation. However, a Democrat -controlled or divided 
Congress may constrain US foreign-policy initiatives through 
tighter budgets, while Republicans could turn less supportive 
as attention shifts to the 2028 presidential race. 
Higher neutral rate: AI investments could raise the neutral 
rate (r*), turning current monetary policy settings 
accommodative. This could necessitate further rate hikes. 
However, the Fed has barely raised its nominal neutral rate 
estimate, lifting it to 3.2% in September from 3.0% a year ago. 
Bottomline: The outlook for rates remains geopolitics 
dependent, with all Fed meetings live . However, based on 
our core scenario, we see the US 10-year yield settling lower 
in the 4.75-5.0% in 6-12 months. A shallow Fed hiking cycle 
due to expected disinflation offers investors a reasonably 
attractive risk-reward in US 10-year government bonds. 
US 30-year government bonds , on the other hand, may 
continue to see elevated yields as the US fiscal deficit is 
unlikely to have improved next year, and may look much 
worse. As a result, “term premium” may keep the ultra -long 
end elevated. Short of some unorthodox policy to artifici ally 
cap them lower, the 30-year yield is likely to continue to drift 
higher towards the 5.5-5.75% range into H2 2027. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist 
Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 697: Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Consumer Checkpoint_ Strength in numbers September saw solid spending across many categories and a strong start to the holidays. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01

13026023     1

Economy 
Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers 
09 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Consumer spending remained very solid in September. Bank of America aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third strongest growth rates over the past four 
years. Month-over-month (MoM) spending was more muted, especially without gas. 
• Spending shows considerable breadth: the vast majority of discretionary retail and services categories are showing growth. 
Moreover, this strength extends across income cohorts, with the ongoing recovery of lower- and middle-income households' 
spending growth. 
• With the winter months fast approaching, holiday spending is already increasing at a solid rate, though it's still early. In Bank of 
America deposit data through September, buy now, pay later (BNPL) usage is rising but not at a rate that is overly concerning. 
However, nearly one in every five households that use BNPL has a repayment of over $500.

Consumer Checkpoint is a regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It aims to provide a holistic and real-time estimate of US 
consumers’ spending and their financial well-being, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America proprietary data. Such data is not 
intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, financial conditions or performance of 
Bank of America. 
A solid September for spending   
Consumer spending continued to look solid in September. Bank of America total aggregated credit and debit card spending per 
household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY), while spending excluding gasoline increased 5.1% YoY. Notably, both are the third 
strongest growth rates in the past four years. Seasonally-adjusted total card spending rose 0.2% month-over-month (MoM), 
while spending excluding gasoline was little changed. Overall, spending growth remains well above the 2025 average rate 
(Exhibit 1). 
Exhibit 1: Total card spending per household rose 6.2% YoY and 5.1% YoY excluding gasoline in September

(monthly, YoY%, non-SA, right-hand side (rhs))

Most spending categories are growing  
One striking thing about recent card spending, in our view, is that it is well balanced across categories. For example, both 
spending on retail (excluding gasoline) and services grew strongly YoY in September, at 3.5% and 5.4%, respectively. Meanwhile, 
retail spending is showing notably better momentum than in 2025 (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: Spending is rising across retail and services 
Retail (excluding gasoline) and services card spending per household (monthly, seasonally adjusted, index January 2025 = 100)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Well balanced across “nice-to-have” and “need-to-have” retail categories  
Is the momentum in retail spending reflective of “nice-to-have” discretionary or “need-to-have” necessity spending? In retail, 
this is a nuanced question, as many categories sit in a gray area between “necessity” and “discretionary”. General merchandise 
stores, for example, sell both essentials like groceries as well as nice-to-have electronics or even jewelry.  
In Exhibit 3, we account for this by grouping retail spending into “more discretionary” (furniture, electronics, building materials, 
online retailers, clothing retailers and miscellaneous stores), “partly discretionary” (auto, health and personal care retailers, 
general merchandise) and “mostly necessity” (gas and groceries). Even within these categories the edges will be blurred, but the 
overall impression is that retail spending growth is widely distributed, with spending rising across all fronts. One caveat: “mostly 
discretionary” spending has been making a smaller contribution since June. 
Exhibit 3: Retail spending is distributed across discretionary and necessity categories  
Percentage points contribution to YoY growth by type of retail spending (three-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data. See methodology for category aggregation definitions.  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Looking at the share of “more discretionary” retail spending categories that are increasing YoY underscores that growth is well 
balanced and broad-based (Exhibit 4). Non-store and online retailers and miscellaneous stores saw some of the fastest growth, 
while furniture and building materials were among the laggards, though other durable categories showed some strength.

For services, growth is driven mostly by discretionary spending  
In services spending, on the other hand, the boundaries between discretionary and necessity spending are often clearer. Travel 
spending (e.g., airlines, lodging) can be thought of as, by and large, discretionary. At the same time, utility bills, rent and 
insurance payments are necessities.  
And in services, discretionary spending remains the clear driver, accounting for over three-quarters of YoY spending growth in 
September (Exhibit 6). Leisure and housing services are major components of growth, though travel and restaurants also made 
positive contributions. However, as we noted last month strength in airline spending largely reflects price rises rather than 
transactions growth (read more in Consumer Checkpoint: Still sizzling).  
Exhibit 6: Services spending growth has been mostly driven by discretionary spending over the past year

Lower-income households’ spending outpaced all but the top 5% 
The breadth in spending growth across categories is also evident across income cohorts. In September, lower-income 
households’ total card spending growth was 5.5% YoY, while the equivalent figures for middle- and higher-income households 
were 4.9% and 4.8% YoY, respectively. The top 5% of households by income continue to show the fastest spending growth, 
5.9% in September, though this has eased somewhat (Exhibit 7). 
Overall, the divergences across income cohorts are much smaller than they were in 2025 and the first three months of this year. 
Just focusing on spending excluding key necessities (gasoline, food and utilities) doesn’t meaningfully change the picture of 
convergence across income cohorts (Exhibit 8).  
Exhibit 7: The top 5% saw the strongest spending growth of any 
income cohort…

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The strength in lower- and middle-income households’ discretionary spending is occurring despite necessity spending shifting 
from a tailwind to a headwind over the past year amid rising gas prices (Exhibit 9). In our view, a key component of why these 
households have been able to combine rising necessity spending with strength in discretionary is the labor market and a 
recovery in their after-tax wage growth (Exhibit 10).  
In September, lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY from 4.7% YoY in August, while growth for middle- and higher-
income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. These changes were modest, and lower-income households are 
continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” pattern we 
observed over much of 2025 and 2026.

Should we be concerned about the easing in household spending and after-tax wage growth for those with the top 5% of 
incomes? For now, probably not, as in our view this spending growth is likely being driven by wealth effects rather than wage 
gains. Absent a sharp correction in equity markets, there is little reason to expect them to retrench their spending. However, 
developments here are worth watching as this cohort comprises a significant proportion of overall consumer spending. They also 
tend to make outsized contributions to several discretionary spending categories (Exhibit 11).  
Exhibit 11: The top 5% of households by income make up a disproportionate amount of spending in highly discretionary categories 
Share of spending by the top 5% of households by income across select discretionary categories (three-month moving average to September 2026, %)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
With summer gone, thoughts turn to the holidays and BNPL… 
Now that summer is over, the next big gauge of consumer momentum is how people spend during the run-up to, and over, the 
holiday season. It is early days, but September spending on holiday items ran well ahead of 2025 (Exhibit 12). Given the backdrop 
of firmer after-tax wage growth and breadth across retail and services categories, this is perhaps not surprising. However, some 
of this spending strength could be due to rising prices as opposed to more purchases.

A concern often raised as we move into the holidays is whether consumers will lean on less traditional forms of credit such as 
buy now, pay later (BNPL) to finance their spending. Frequent BNPL users tend to have higher credit card utilization rates (read 
more in: Do consumers have wiggle room to absorb higher gas prices?).  
Looking at the share of households in Bank of America deposit data with a BNPL payment, we do tend to see a seasonal rise 
over the holidays (Exhibit 13), though in past years, these increases have quickly reversed. Further, while the underlying trend in 
BNPL usage is upwards, there are no signs of a sharp acceleration. We do, however, see some evidence that BNPL is being used 
to finance more expensive purchases, with the share of households making repayments above $500 rising, albeit relatively slowly 
(Exhibit 14).  
Exhibit 13: 8.3% of Bank of America households had a BNPL

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: October 26-30

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD:
October 26-30
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 24 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Monica Sauro / Unsplash

### Page 3

The S&P 500 fell 0.5% last week, and the Nasdaq dropped 0.7%, both
posting second straight weekly losses. The Nasdaq’s 2.9% slide on
Thursday was its biggest one-day drop since June, as concerns flared
over the cost and availability of financial capital for AI investment. The
price of WTI crude topped $100 a barrel amid US-Iran conflict concerns,
while the 10-year Treasury yield fell to 5.0%.
The FOMC meeting (Wed) is the focus this week, alongside the release of

Q3 GDP and September's PCED inflation report (Thu). San Francisco Fed
President Mary Daly and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem speak
on Friday. A heavy week of Q3 earnings reports rounds out a packed
calendar. Here’s more:
(1) FOMC. The FOMC’s two-day meeting concludes Wednesday, followed
by Fed Chair Kevin Warsh’s press conference. The CME FedWatch Tool
puts the odds of a hike this week at 9% and the odds of one in December
at 84%. Federal funds futures imply 2.7 rate hikes over the next 12

months and 1.4 over the next six (chart). We believe the committee should
hike on Wednesday but won't because the midterm elections are so close.

### Page 4

(2) GDP & PCED. The first estimate of Q3 GDP (Thu) follows Q2’s third
estimate of 2.2% saar, with the GDP price index up 6.1%. The Atlanta
Fed’s GDPNow model estimates Q3 growth at 3.5% as of October 20, led
by a 17.8% jump in business equipment spending (chart).
September’s core PCED (Thu) follows August’s 3.0% y/y. That was well
below September’s 4.6% pace of core PPI final demand for personal
consumption and above the core CPI’s 2.6% (chart). The Cleveland Fed’s

Inflation Nowcasting model projects September’s core and headline PCED
at 3.1% and 3.6% y/y, up from August’s 3.0% and 3.4%. (The model’s
m/m rates are 0.28% and 0.47%.)
CPI computer software and accessories rose 28.4% y/y in September and
PPI electronic components 26.8%, while PCED computers and peripheral

### Page 5

equipment rose just 8.4% in August. September’s PCED will show
whether that gap starts to close.
(3) ADP & unemployment claims. ADP’s weekly employment reading
(Tue) follows a drop in the four-week average to 18,500 for the week
ended October 3, down from 26,000 the prior week (chart). That’s still
more than double July’s low of 8,250, pointing to a solid October payrolls
report.
Initial jobless claims (Thu) follow last week’s 194,000, below expectations

and near the lowest level since the late 1960s. The four-week average
stands at 197,800 (chart). Continuing claims rose to 1,728,000 for the
week ended October 9, up from 1,710,000, while their four-week average
edged down to 1,708,000.

### Page 6

(4) Regional business surveys. The Richmond Fed’s manufacturing
index (Tue) follows September’s -2 reading. The regional M-PMI business
activity index slipped to 13.4 in September from August’s 18.7, while the
national M-PMI eased to 54.5 from 54.6 (chart).
(5) Earnings. The earnings reporting season heats up this week, with
36% of the S&P 500 companies set to report. Boeing, Seagate, and
Corning report on Tuesday. Alphabet, Microsoft, SK Hynix, and GE

Vernova follow on Wednesday. Samsung, Intel, Eli Lilly, SanDisk, and
Amazon report on Thursday. Exxon Mobil reports on Friday.
Analysts’ consensus estimates imply Q3-2026 operating EPS growth for
S&P 500 companies of 24.1% y/y, slightly up from last week (chart). Q4
estimates have also risen.

### Page 7

(6) Global central banks. Both the Reserve Bank of New Zealand and
the Bank of Canada (Wed) are expected to hike by 25bps. The markets
are pricing in 88% odds that the RBNZ will lift its official cash rate to
3.00% and an 85% chance that the BOC will raise its policy rate to 2.50%.
The European Central Bank (Thu) and the Bank of Japan (Fri) are set to
hold their policy rates at 2.50% and 1.25%, respectively (chart).
December is the focus for the latter pair. The markets are pricing in 75%

odds of an ECB hike in December and a 74% probability of a BOJ hike.
Guidance from ECB President Christine Lagarde and BOJ Governor
Kazuo Ueda will show whether those odds hold.

圖表一：衛星產業市場規模

資料來源： SIA ，中信投顧整理  
Falcon 9 開啟火箭技術革命 ： 傳統 火箭多為一次性 設計 ，主要結構於
發 射後 拋棄， 使每 次發射 任務 均須重 新製 造與整 備， 導致 火 箭發 射成
本高昂。而 SpaceX 的獵鷹 9 號(Falcon 9 )於 2015 年首次完成一級火箭
回收，並於 2017 年實現回收助推器再次飛行，突破火箭一次性使用限
制，降低火箭發射成本， 使衛星寬頻通訊商業化可行性提高。 Falcon 9
每次發射衛星 報價 約在 3,200 美元 /公斤 ，SpaceX 自用成本更低 ，而最
新 的傳 統火 箭(Ariane 6) 發射 報價 則 約在 6,000 美元 /公斤 ， 相比 之 下
SpaceX 在火箭發射成本上有顯著優勢。 而 下 一 代 火 箭星艦 (Starship)
推測每公斤成本約在 600 美元 /公斤， 且目標 為全部回收並 將發射成本
進一步降低至 200 美元 /公斤 ，有望進一步帶動衛星發射數量 。

## Document 790: The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco_223e3a6a.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13025075     1

Economy 
The Institute Employment Report: September 2026 
07 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job growth softened further in September, according to Bank of America's deposit data, with growth of 1.4% year-over-year 
(YoY), from 1.5% in August. But falling unemployment payments in September indicate a relatively healthy overall picture. 
• Lower-income households' wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap. After-tax wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY 
for lower-income households in September, while rising to 3.8% and 3.7% for middle- and higher-income households, 
respectively. 
• Lower tax withholdings and a relative strengthening in the younger/lower-income end of the labor market are driving this story. 
Additionally, average hours have risen in some lower-paid industries, which is also boosting pay packets.

Job growth slowed in September, but unemployment trends are benign  
Our estimate of job growth – which uses Bank of America deposit data to track changes in the number of customer accounts 
receiving a paycheck (see Methodology) – slowed further in September, to 1.4% year-over-year (YoY) from 1.5% YoY in August. 
The data can be noisy, partly due to seasonal variation and differences in pay-period timing, but in our view, the latest reading is 
consistent with the easing in payrolls growth seen in the September Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (Exhibit 1). 
Still, despite slower hiring, there is little sign of a rise in joblessness in Bank of America data. Unemployment payments into 
customer accounts showed a YoY decline in September (Exhibit 2). So, overall, labor market trends appear resilient.   
Exhibit 1: September payroll growth showed further slowing in 
Bank of America deposit account data  
Payroll estimates from Bank of America customer deposit account data 
(three-month moving average, % YoY), the Bureau of Labor Statistics 
(BLS) and Automatic Data Processing (ADP) (monthly, YoY)

Source: Bank of America internal data, Haver Analytics 
Note: BLS and ADP data are seasonally adjusted, Bank of America data is not seasonally 
adjusted.

After-tax wage growth cooled for lower-income, but rose for other cohorts 
Lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY in September from 4.7% YoY in August, while wage growth for middle- and 
higher-income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. Overall, these changes were modest, and lower-income 
households are continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” 
pattern we observed over much of 2025 and 2026 (Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap 
After-tax wage and salary growth by household income terciles, based on Bank of America aggregated consumer deposit account data (three-month moving 
average, YoY%, SA)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
In our view, the pick-up in lower- and middle-income households’ after-tax wage growth continues to reflect the impact of the 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) in reducing tax withholdings, alongside an underlying rise in job-to-job changes at the 
younger/lower-income end of the labor market (for more, read: The Institute Employment Report: August 2026). Consistent with 
that underlying pick up, BLS data shows a solid YoY rise in average hours worked in several relatively lower-paid industries, such 
as manufacturing, retail and other services. Hourly workers in these industries are likely seeing rising paychecks as a result 
(Exhibit 4).    
Exhibit 4: Some relatively lower-paid industries have seen a rise in hours worked  
Average weekly hours by industry, July-September 2026 (% YoY) and average hourly earnings by industry, July-September 2026 ($)

Source: Haver Analytics

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Estimate of payroll growth from Bank of America internal data is based on the change in customer accounts receiving a paycheck in the month. 
An adjustment is made for the difference between overall population growth and customer account growth. Occasionally historic payments can 
be reclassified, leading to revisions in the series.  
An estimate of bonus growth from Bank of America deposit data is calculated by looking at customers who have received an inbound ACH 
payroll transaction in the last two years. From this sample an estimate of bonuses is derived by looking for payroll transactions which are over 
50% higher than the median regular payroll payments received by the customer. Of these payments only those that were received around the 
same time in each of the last two years are selected.  
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

(+84) 24-7303-5333  kbsv.research@kbsec.com.vn

19/10/2026

Nguồn: V_KBSource

Triển vọng kinh tế 4Q2026 
Giữ nhịp tăng trưởng, vững vàng về đích

Một số dự báo chính của KBSV về kinh tế Việt Nam trong năm 2026:

1) Tăng trưởng GDP năm 2026 được dự báo sẽ đạt 9.0%, với động lực chính 
đến từ định hướng đẩy mạnh giải ngân đầu tư công của Chính phủ, dòng 
vốn FDI duy trì tích cực và hoạt động sản xuất công nghiệp phục hồi theo 
xuất khẩu. Về rủi ro, chúng tôi cho rằng Việt Nam sẽ tiếp tục đối mặt với 
một số yếu tố bất định từ bên ngoài, bao gồm: rủi ro xung đột Mỹ – Iran 
kéo dài hơn dự kiến, xu hướng tăng của mặt bằng lãi suất toàn cầu gây gia 
tăng áp lực tài chính và kéo giảm tổng cầu, cùng nguy cơ vỡ bong bóng AI.

2) Chúng tôi nâng dự báo lạm phát bình quân năm 2026 của Việt Nam lên 
mức 4.7% YoY, cao hơn mục tiêu kiểm soát lạm phát của Chính phủ, dựa 
trên kịch bản dự báo thận trọng hơn về diễn biến xung đột địa chính trị và 
giá năng lượng thế giới trong ngắn hạn.

3) Mặt bằng lãi suất trong quý IV được dự báo sẽ ổn định quanh vùng hiện 
tại và không xuất hiện thêm nhịp tăng đột biến, nhờ dòng tiền cải thiện 
từ giải ngân đầu tư công và kỳ vọng có thêm các biện pháp hỗ trợ thanh 
khoản trung dài hạn từ phía Chính phủ và NHNN.

4) Tỷ giá USD/VND trong quý cuối năm có thể chịu một số sức ép do Fed dự 
báo còn thêm một lần tăng lãi suất nữa trong năm. Dù vậy, chúng tôi cho 
rằng tỷ giá USD/VND khó tăng mạnh mà chỉ quay lại mức đầu năm, dựa 
trên kỳ vọng chỉ số DXY không biến động mạnh, nguồn cung ngoại tệ cải 
thiện (FDI giải ngân, kiều hối, nâng hạng TTCK) và chênh lệch lãi suất 
VND–USD duy trì trạng thái dương.
